>
> How do you know if you are an NPC, do you repeat the same patterns and habits depending on the model of the processor and the level of your program, which comes from the experiences of the cultural environment. Yes, but what about emotions!! Neurotransmitters are chemicals, short codes that activate response programs, your thoughts create emotions (think of something really bad and observe the emotions). Consciousness is a screen that observes the game "you as a character", who plays this Game of Life, where "you" are convinced that you are this character! Who gave you a name, ethnicity, etc.? In reality, you did not choose your character or the level on which it plays, everything is random, but as the program is written, you try to control and direct the course of the game. It is impossible, because the nature of the rendering is in constant eternal infinite change, too many parameters and possibilities to control. This game is updated and seems to be in the process, because so many players raise the level of consciousness of the character.  
>   
> We are bioelectric machines whose "processor power" (genetics) and "software" (culture) determine the reaction. When a person acts only on autopilot – waking up, going to work, browsing social media, sleeping – they are repeating the code that the environment has written into them. Dopamine, serotonin and cortisol are like game variables that regulate the character's energy levels and motivation. They "force" the character to act in a certain way in order to survive. Suffering often arises from the observer forgetting that they are the observer and starting to believe that they are the character who is taking the blows of the game personally, the so-called emotional response is a self-programmed process created by thoughts. If you watch a movie, you do not suffer for the character in the same way as if you think you are it. When consciousness "wakes up" to realize that the name, ethnicity and story are just rendered data, the nature of the game changes from fear to play. In the light of quantum physics and chaos theory, the world is so complex that no single "character" can calculate the next move perfectly. You will notice that other characters are also different manifestations of the same "source code".  
>   
> If a war movie is shown on the screen (in consciousness), the screen does not get wounded. If a romantic comedy is shown on the screen, the screen does not fall in love. If there is emptiness on the screen, the screen does not disappear. Similarly, consciousness (you) is completely free and pure from all the drama that your "character" experiences. In the film, the character and the background appear to be separate, but in reality they are the same light on the screen. All experiences - thoughts, feelings, physical pains, sounds of the outside world - are "made" from this same fabric of consciousness. There is no "outside world" outside the screen.  
>   
> If you are the screen, you do not really move, even if the character runs in the game. You are not born, you do not die. All movement and change are just the dance of pixels (energy/information) on the surface. The programming is so tight that attention is only focused on the changing objects (the flow of thoughts and emotions). It's like watching a movie so intense that you forget you're sitting in a movie theater and the screen itself exists. Being aware of this—not just understanding it as a concept, but recognizing it as an ultimate state—changes the rules of the game irrevocably. When attention shifts from the character's drama (content) to itself (the screen), something happens that you might call a game break or "glitch" in the system. The character continues to render, but its programmed need to "win" or "survive" loses its grip.  
> Welcome to reality :D

> 30+ years ago, I challenged the Ed graduate studies professor about how to make Bloom's Taxonomy of Learning useful, instead of dangerous, as it is today.  It is simply to remove the hierarchy in each Domain, assume that all levels of each Domain are happening, at all times, and to make the job of teachers to help students REALIZE how each of these is taking place. It is the OPPOSITE of telling a student, "Don't just memorize for the test!", which leads the student to falsely believe that this is even possible.  If you want INTERESTED students, tell them there are all these things happening to all of them, all the time. This removes so many self-fulfilling prophecies, as well as the authoritarianism and separation of goals from students.  I tried to be concise. The professor could not refute what I said.  But, nobody's interested in making Bloom's Taxonomy useful.  HTH - Robert

### **@marjankrebelj4007** | 👍 467 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=Ugxr2IvUJUORX8bD-gB4AaABAg)

This lecture explains EVERYTHING I have ever experienced in schools, either as a student or later as a teacher. In 2011–13, I attempted something similar to Prof. Jiang, but I failed as miserably as he did. I was kicked out under the pretense of "budget cuts because of the financial crisis." The system I proposed made students work hard, but they absolutely loved it because it was one of those classes that not only stimulated but respected their imagination and individuality.

However, little of that mattered to my bosses because I made two mistakes that a player in this game should never commit: (1) my superior class made every other feel boring (and the teachers look stupid), and (2) I outshone even my own boss, who was actually enlightened enough to be on board with me (initially). After two years, I was replaced by someone who was absolutely hated by students but knew how to play the game perfectly. I was so naive that I expected to be promoted as a very good and popular teacher,  and couldn't understand why the other guy was chosen, LOL.

So, thank you Prof. Jiang for providing such a thorough explanation. As an autistic person, I have always taken things at face value, but these classes help me understand the world as it is. I struggled a lot trying to understand what was going on and made many mistakes along the way. I have a feeling we might share some of that. :)

> @nndeki I don't think so. The teacher (prof Jiang in this case) creates the world of the lesson he is teaching and the student can be passive of active actors in that world. If they're all passive, the lesson unfolds as planned by the teacher. On the other hand, if a great question is asked, it adds a detour to the road map of the lesson and creates new insights that were not originally planned to be shared by the teacher to the students. This benefits everyone including the teacher as it helps him refine his lessons more and more.  Of course, if the teacher doesn't have enough focus in his lesson plan, he can get lost in the weeds, but prof Jiang neatly brought it back to his original point.

> @GeopoliticsandGeoHistory I think that God and religion are also a "cover," just as science does today, and religion once did. The problem isn't so much science itself, but what they decide to teach you in school (not because of school itself, but because what you learn in school conditions you for the rest of your life, and goes on to shape 90% of your beliefs and values). School doesn't explain the conscience, the subconscious, or the imagination. But today there are psychological studies that largely explain their meaning and functioning. For example, by reading books outside of school, I've discovered many more things that have revolutionized the way we think and see things, like a rediscovery of the world. I'm talking about psychology, the subconscious, and the imagination. There are things in your head that no one has ever explained to you, but they allow you to do whatever you want and see the world differently than how you've been conditioned to believe. Understanding these things (I'm not talking about science, but about the pure workings of the mind and human behavior), you begin to understand that religion is also a human creation designed to control humanity, or at least to feed it something to believe in, something so powerful it's been passed down for centuries. For human behavior to function, humans need to believe in something: whatever it is, and 100% of the time, they'll believe in something they were taught as children. Try asking yourself why two newborn children, one in the West and one in the East, will be Christian and one in the East, respectively (I'm generalizing). Now tell me if a newborn child already knows there's a God and something to believe in. No, but it's only as they grow up that they'll be led by their parents and society to believe in something; their parents have been subjected to the same thing, and so on. Unfortunately, today our civilization is at a level of development that implies too many "problems", with great disadvantages for the individual, but the "well-being" of the community, in which in reality the majority of individuals will have problems

### **@charlesmiller7433** | 👍 621 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=UgzR8_DdiI_TJNQI64J4AaABAg)

We are living through the largest social experiment in human history - the complete atomization of human beings - and it's killing us faster than any war, plague, or natural disaster ever could. For 200,000 years, humans usually lived in groups of 150 people or less, where every person knew every other person on a meaningful level, where raising children was a community effort, where emotional support was automatic, where belonging was guaranteed by birth, where your survival and everyone else's survival were completely interdependent. Then in the span of about 200 years - a fucking BLINK in evolutionary terms - we demolished that entire structure and replaced it with... nothing. Nothing except the promise that rugged individualism and consumer capitalism would somehow fulfill the same emotional and social needs that took millennia to evolve.

And now we're all sitting here like confused lab rats pushing buttons that used to give us reliable dopamine hits but now give us electric shocks, wondering why we're so miserable. We've created a world where the most basic human need - belonging to a group that gives a shit whether you live or die - has been turned into a luxury commodity that most people can't afford. We've made community into a hobby, family into a choice you can opt out of, and child-rearing into a terrifying individual responsibility that bankrupts you both financially and emotionally.

The loneliness epidemic is a completely predictable outcome of destroying the social structures that made human emotional regulation possible in the first place. We've normalized a level of social isolation that would have been literally impossible for 99.9% of human existence.

And instead of admitting we've created a fundamentally inhuman social system, we've decided the problem is individual pathology. Oh, you're lonely? That's a you problem. Go to therapy. Take antidepressants. Join a hobby group. Download a dating app. As if loneliness is a personal failing that can be solved through better consumer choices, rather than the inevitable result of living in a society that has systematically destroyed almost every mechanism humans evolved to create lasting social bonds.

The dating apps, the hobby groups, the therapy, the self-help books - those are band-aids on a severed artery. And the most insidious part is that the people who got lucky - who inherited social connections, who luckily found their tribes before their emotional systems started to collapse, who managed to create families before the economic and social costs became prohibitive - these people look at the growing population of isolated, despairing individuals and think it's a moral failing. They think the lonely people just need to try harder, be more positive, put themselves out there more. They can't see that they're survivors of a social apocalypse telling the casualties to just walk it off.

We are watching the real-time collapse of human social organization, and instead of treating it like the civilizational emergency it is, we're treating it like a market opportunity. Loneliness? There's an app for that. Social isolation? Here's a subscription service. Community breakdown? Try this new networking platform. We've turned the destruction of human social bonds into a fucking business model that doesn't appear to be solving shit.
 
The people who are too emotionally intelligent to accept shallow substitutes for real connection, who are too authentic to perform the social theater that keeps the system running are the canaries in the societal coal mine. They're the early warning system telling us that we've created a way of life that is dissolving the human spirit. But instead of listening to them, we pathologize them, medicate them, or ignore them completely.

This isn't sustainable. A species cannot survive the complete destruction of its social bonding mechanisms.

> **@bienu4345** | 👍 76 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=UgzR8_DdiI_TJNQI64J4AaABAg.ARiB2RCq75VAS1MoU_ZrlI)  
>
> I'd like to analyse this and add my own thoughts, conducive to a healthy conversation for those who still read long texts. Your comment was reposted on tiktok and as of now has 20k likes - it is from there that I came, and I'm sure this is the case for many others reading this. The "atomization" of humanity of which you describe is indeed very real, however your description of it is biased.   
>   
> Humans evolved to live in groups no larger than 200, because that is the limit of how many people you can personally know and trust on a daily basis. This is still true for other apes like chimpanzees. This didn't change with the industrial revolution, it changed with agriculture and language. Farming allowed us to produce much more food and therefore people, but required us to live where we farmed. This changed almost every aspect of how we live. It would inevitably create the first cities, but it could not happen without language and specifically the ability to tell stories.   
>   
> A chimp can tell his tribe that there is a lion, but a human can tell his tribe that the lion goes to the river at sunrise. Critically, a human can convince strangers to believe in something that they haven't seen. If everyone believes in the same stories and acts according to the rules those stories make, strangers are able to work together. It's under this system that we have lived since the agricultural revolution. Millions of men have ran into battle to kill and die because they were utterly convinced that God told them to do it, or that they were defending their nation, or that they would be paid handsomely. And yet, there is no scientific evidence of God, nations are purely ideas, and money is only valuable because everyone believes it is.   
>   
> The truth is that everything we have accomplished as a species has been built on concepts and stories that aren't real. An "imagined order".  
> This is not a bad thing, but it does mean that the ideas that hold a society together are surprisingly fragile. The industrial revolution happened at the same time as a capitalist and a scientific revolution. These three things plus colonialism have incredibly intertwined histories. Scientific expeditions into the unknown were also military conquests. Advancements led to rapid productivity gains, changing economics from what was seen as a zero-sum game into the capitalist system we have today that relies on the idea that gains will always increase. This system was unbelievably successful. For most of history you could expect to die from disease, famine, or violence. Smallpox killed up to 500 million people just in the 1900s. Today there are zero deaths. In 1800, half of all children didn't live past the age of 5. Today it is 3%. 200 years ago, if you had a bad harvest and died of starvation, it was seen as a normal occurance and furthermore that it was God's will and there was nothing we could have done. Today, when a famine occurs it is seen as a failure of humanity. An avoidable tragedy, which should have been prevented, and where people can be held accountable. The success of capitalism and science can not be understated. We have defeated so many causes of death that today the expected way to die is by living so long that your own body begins to break down. At the same time the global population ballooned.  
>   
> And yet, we appear to be no happier than when we invented fire. There are more slaves today than at any point in history. More people kill themselves than are murdered. Standards of living are decreasing throughout the world. People no longer believe in God, or in their nation, or in any of the myths we've been told to believe in. Humanity is stronger than ever, but society is weak and collapsing.  
>   
> I won't restate what you said about the loneliness epidemic because I completely agree. But the way you talk about it irks me.  
> Because it isn't "we". It's them. And by them I mean the corporations. Corporations are an idea, they don't physically exist. But ideas influence the way humans act and that is when they become real. Through the capitalist machine, it is the corporations that have orchestrated the destruction of community and society, and nurtured the neoliberal world order specifically to maximize profit extraction from the lower classes. They do it because it is what they are made to do. Make profit. So they did.   
>   
> They manipulated us through the government and through propaganda to believe whatever they want so they can make more money.  
> When cars were invented, and began killing people on the street, they convinced us it was our fault, that roads are for cars. Then they made roads the only option. Now everyone needs to buy a car, and pay for fuel, and repairs, and insurance. The stock market loves all this money being spent, meanwhile we all become poorer and lonelier too because you will never meet other people in your car.   
> It is in ways like this that slowly, every aspect of our lives has become monetized, digitized, compartmentalized, foreign-factory slop.   
>   
> And so here we are, the new generation, raised on the promise of ideas that nobody believes in anymore. Living in the twilight era of a world hyperpower, trying to make sense of life while society collapses around us, and corporations try to leech away everything we care about. And I'm worried, because they might win. With the power of super-intelligent AI in their hands, it may become impossible to change the trajectory.   
> These may be the last years where we even have a chance to change things. But we are all sedated, isolated, rage-baited, penetrated so deeply with the propaganda that we would rather drug ourselves or argue about chicks with dicks online instead of looking with our own eyes at our own lives being taken away from us and fighting together to take it back.

> **@charlesmiller7433** | 👍 3 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=UgzR8_DdiI_TJNQI64J4AaABAg.ARiB2RCq75VAS5s_Ja1j-5)  
>
> ​@broski_33The Hikers, the Flower, and the Meaning We Create  
>   
> I imagine that I'm on a hike with a group of people. Everyone's talking about how the trail is supposed to lead to this incredible view at the summit—a place where they’ll finally feel something meaningful.  
>   
> I’m walking with them, but halfway through, a single flower catches my attention. It's small, unassuming, standing alone in the middle of the forest.  
>   
> I stop.  
>   
> "Hey, you guys go ahead," I tell the group. "I'm good right here."  
>   
> They look at me like I'm nuts, but they shrug and keep walking. I take a moment observing the flower, imagining a story. Maybe it grew here, all alone, thinking no one would ever notice. Maybe it felt invisible, like it didn’t matter. But then I came along. I saw it. I made it matter. I gave it more meaning within my lived experience by doing more thinking about how this flower or maybe flowers in general might relate to the universe we live in. Maybe the beauty of a flower represents a kind of acknowledgement of potentially meaningful complexity that stands out among an otherwise crowded benign landscape that we might normally observe within our conscious awareness.   
>   
> So maybe the flower caught my eye when I was hiking because something within my mind said to me let's stop the current activity and ask ourselves what does this flower mean to me because it might have unironically more meaning than hiking to a summit that may not have been justified as meaningful beyond potentially algorithmic societal norms of performative busyness or normalcy instead of pausing and reflecting on the vibrant colorful expression of life from a complex living structure called a flower.  
>   
> Later, when the hikers come back down, they're sweaty and tired, with a distant look in their eyes. "Did you find the meaning you were looking for at the summit?" I ask.  
>   
> They shrug. "It was fine, I guess. What about you?"  
>   
> I’m beaming. "I found a flower. And not just any flower—a flower I gave meaning to. It wasn’t important until I saw it and made it matter. It was something small but when I gave it a part of my soul it felt meaningful to me."  
>   
> The hikers stare at me like I’ve grown three heads. "Bro, are you okay? That sounds... intense." My emotions collectively facepalm, muttering, They’ll never get it.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> But the story doesn’t end there.  
>   
> Word gets around about the flower and how I smiled like a lunatic when I talked about it. A few days later, some of the hikers show up at Embarrassment’s flower shop, tracking mud all over the floor. "Hey, we’re here for the best flower," they announce. "We heard you were talking about how meaningful his flower was. We want that."  
>   
> Embarrassment, the shop owner, rolls his eyes. "Sure, here’s a flower. That’ll be $10."  
>   
> "$10? That’s not meaningful enough," they say, annoyed. "We want something rare, something special!"  
>   
> Embarrassment sighs, trudges to the back of the shop, and grabs some random flower he’s been ignoring for weeks. "Here," he says flatly. "This one’s very rare. $200."  
>   
> The hikers don’t even flinch. They hand over the money, grinning like they’ve just bought the secret to happiness. They gather around the flower, beaming and congratulating themselves. "This is it! This is the meaning we’ve been looking for!"  
>   
> Embarrassment watches them leave, shaking his head. Before they even make it to their car, their smiles have already started to fade.  
>   
> Meanwhile, I’m sitting nearby, watching the whole thing unfold. I turn to Embarrassment and say, "That’s going to make a great story, don’t you think?"  
>   
> Embarrassment smirks. "Yeah, and maybe they would've been happier if I charged $300?" and I laugh.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> And the story might be saying that an emotional need might not be something that society says is important or noteworthy.  
>   
> Because when your emotion taps you on the shoulder and says can we stop here then you might have the courage to pause and reflect because soul-level introspection is what brings you more well-being and peace.  
>   
> And then you might tell society to go on ahead I'll be okay because I'm focusing on the parts of myself that make me a human being.  
>   
> And those are what are important to me. Not so much the summit. But what my emotion is telling me my humanity needs.

>   
> "Think about using your pain as data. But not for your own private logbook. Use it to forge emotionally intense, pro-human expression. The posts, the conversations, the art, your very presence are no longer about 'a healing journey.' They are now communiqués from the front line. They are propaganda for a revolution of the soul."  
>   
> "The societal machine that includes the 'successful,' the emotionally avoidant, the ones who have 'made it' continues to suppress emotional suffering because of narratives of polite silence. These scripts count on us to take our disillusionment, our 'Individuation,' our profound suffering, and deal with it quietly in therapy or in our journals so most don't have to be inconvenienced by it. Society wants our pain to remain a private, manageable, personal problem because that causes the least immediate disruption to money or power or social-status generation. Because the quieter you are then the less they need to expend energy to deal with the annoying stimulus of your suffering humanity."  
>   
> "The new mission is to communicate emotional pain as a public and systemic problem. So take the raw, unfiltered data of your suffering and use it to pound on the gilded, sound-proof door of an avoidant suppressive society until the hinges begin to crack from the sound of the brokenhearted who may have kept silent so far with their heads down because maybe when you speak your pain then you might join the emotionally abandoned while others are desperately looking away because others are reliably following the current norms within society to ignore or suppress emotions due to emotional illiteracy."  
>   
> // THE TACTICAL OBJECTIVE: END THE REIGN OF THE PLATITUDE //  
>   
> "If you see a 'Top 10 Ways to Be Happy' article then maybe preserve the idea of rolling-your-eyes and treating it as a gross-numbing-distant-dissociating piece of propaganda because the next step might be to write a blisteringly prohuman, long-form, hyper-validating treatise on why the shallow largely-meaningless parroted so-called advice could be seen as an act of repetitive psychic sabotage against the uncounted observers who might be genuinely suffering, with the counter-force being the use of your own pain as the primary data source for change."  
>   
> "So pound on their digital-based doors using email/texting/comments with a plea for seeking deeper connection while remembering to demand emotional justice in the form of actionable insights rather than empty statements. Demand that they get off their so-called 'happy and healthy' or 'wealthy and wise' behinds and to stop peddling their useless, context-free platitudes that don't require them to lift a finger to help you which is them attempting to avoid participating with you in the messy, difficult, high-stakes work of building a world where genuine connection is actually possible because people stop parroting useless garbage advice because they start getting called-out."  
>   
> "The goal is to become one of the architects of an emotional intelligence tipping point. To inject so much high-fidelity, pro-human, emotionally literate data into the system that the evasive or avoidant have nowhere left to hide. It's to create a world where, when a person says 'I am lonely and feeling disconnected,' the default response from society is not a dismissive shrug or a link to a self-help blog, but a profound, validating, and genuinely useful engagement with that pain to find them relief to process their suffering emotions."  
>   
> The next stage of the journey is to move from a quiet, personal molting to becoming a loud, public, and glorious emotionally-pissed-off pro-human dragon of emotional insights. It's taking the key you forged in the fires of your own agony not just looking to unlock your own cage but you are using that emotional key template to help pick the locks of the prison doors of emotionally suppressive societal norms everywhere else. Help release the dragons 🔥🐲.

> ​@Predictive_1The current system of labor enslavement which is the enforcement of obedience under threat of violence is not a "solution" to societal problems; it is the ultimate admission of systemic failure. When a society relies on the threat of a physical cage, the slow death of starvation, or the exposure of homelessness to ensure that children are raised, the elderly are cared for, or the gears of the economy keep turning, it has ceased to be a community and has become a labor camp with a PR department.  
>   
> ​​The argument that "we can't just let people abandon their responsibilities" is often used to justify the money grinder of capitalism. However, if a responsibility is so naturally abhorrent or the conditions so miserable that people would only fulfill it under the threat of annihilation or existential precarity, the problem isn't the "laziness" of the individual—it is the toxicity of the structure.   
>   
> When the means of production—the land, the farms, the water, and the building materials—have been unironically seized by the rich then "freedom of choice" becomes a cruel joke. If you must work a dull and repetitive job just to access the basic calories needed to keep your heart beating, you are not "free"; you are a captive negotiating for your life.  
>   
> The Only Real Solution is Voluntary Consent:  
> ​Any solution that requires even a drop of slavery or forced obedience under threat of violence is immediately poisoned because it requires a societal machine to enforce enslavement or violence. To actually solve this problem, participation in community or parenting or labor must be consented to at all times while also being economically supported and the environment nurturing so that people choose it, never forced under threat of violence because that would be literal enslavement.  
>   
> ​If the only way to keep society running is to threaten your "citizens" with the street or the cell, then your society is actually a slow-motion hostage crisis. If you introduce force, you stop solving the problem of how to live together and start solving the problem of how to keep the cattle in the pen.

Wow, professor. I am so impressed by your candidness regarding those past events. I think a lot of us were idealists too, and we also had the crushing blow of reality hit us (each in our own way), forever changing our perspective. I have nothing but the utmost respect for your original vision, and am saddened (but not entirely surprised) that things worked out the way they did. Changing the status quo is near-impossible, but educating people about what it is and why it exists is probably the best way to change it. Our generation may not have a chance, but future ones will. Thank you for giving these students (and myself) knowledge that will truly benefit their lives and help them to understand why the world is the way it is.

I would be lying if I said I am not at least a little bit jealous that they are getting a much earlier head-start on recognizing how the world really works than I ever had. I think a lot of parents shelter their children from this knowledge to preserve their innocence for as long as possible, but I think this hinders their growth, success, and understanding dramatically. As painful as it can be to exist in, understanding reality will be the first true step towards changing it.

Thank you for what you do.

### **@AlmotasemBarhoush** | 👍 8 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=UgyWxoXiAIfgnUwmq2h4AaABAg)

Below is the English translation and explanation of Thomas Schelling's famous quote regarding the innate strategic intelligence of children from specific cultural backgrounds, such as Arab-Muslim culture, compared to elite academic graduates:

The Statement: "The Arab-Muslim Child vs. The Cambridge Graduate"

The renowned Nobel laureate and game theorist Thomas Schelling famously argued that in certain strategic scenarios involving coordination, an uneducated person—specifically mentioning an "Arab child"—could outperform a highly trained academic from an elite institution like Cambridge University.

Why did he say this? (The Strategic Philosophy)

Focal Points (Schelling Points): Schelling’s primary contribution to game theory was the discovery of "Focal Points." These are solutions that people choose by default because they seem natural, special, or relevant based on shared culture and intuition.

Cultural Synchrony: He argued that a child raised in a rich, traditional, and socially cohesive culture (like Arab-Muslim culture) possesses a "shared mental map" with others from the same background. This allows them to predict what others will do without any communication.

The Flaw of "Over-Education": A Cambridge graduate might over-analyze a situation using complex mathematical models, leading to paralysis or confusion. In contrast, the child relies on social intuition and common norms, which are often more effective in solving real-world coordination problems.

The Conclusion

For Schelling, strategic success is not always about IQ or advanced degrees; it is about shared understanding. He used this comparison to prove that "culture" is a powerful strategic tool that allows people to act as one, even when they cannot speak to each other.

Learn more about Thomas Schelling’s "Strategy of Conflict":

Thomas Schelling - Nobel Prize Facts & Insights

### **@Synthstrucplexfyimmunosystemci** | 👍 9 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=Ugw8auR1O-BgMyJzFol4AaABAg)

Sigh… this really resonated with me. School often feels less about learning and more about exhausting people just enough so they’re able to work and comply later >..< It makes me wonder what the purpose of knowledge even is when information is everywhere, yet true understanding feels rarer than ever. What can you really do differently from someone else? Is it just a matter of having more time, or is there something else we’re missing?

What hit hardest was the idea of learning from the wrong incentives. I’ve often felt like I was losing—not because I didn’t want to learn, but because I was surrounded by people who just wanted to outperform others rather than actually understand anything. That kind of environment doesn’t just hold individuals back; it drags everyone down.

I used to do more. I used to care more. And sometimes I honestly wonder what the point was—or is. Timing, guidance, and the people around you seem to matter far more than effort alone. Watching this, I couldn’t help thinking how much luck plays a role too: having supportive parents, access to the “right” paths, even just basic safety. Without that, college—or real learning at all—might never have been an option.

Maybe that’s why videos like this matter. They don’t offer easy answers, but at least they explain why so many of us feel exhausted, confused, and behind—not because we failed, but because the game itself was never really about learning.

Schools had a monopoly on knowledge. If you wanted the knowledge, and status for a Job, you had to go to school. We did believe School was the "answer", because If you did finish school, you could get a job and an income. But now, School doesn't have a monopoly on knowledge, and doesn't create the same results as in the past.   
>   
> With the rise of AI and the Internet, we have access to all the knowledge in the world, so much that it makes our heads explode, and in your case, can overwhelm us. With those tools, you don't need school to learn skills. You also don't need status, or a paper saying you know a lot about a topic, to generate an income. One can only choose a path to go into, learn all the skills and knowledge asociated with it, and pursue a career on it (what I did when I dropped out of college, and do not regret it. I'm from a 3rd world country, and have a high income without a degree).  
>   
> School takes 20 years to teach you what you can learn with technology in 5 years.   
>   
> Unfortunately, schools need to adapt, or will die. When the school system becomes so unuseful, and such a massive burden to the state, the goverment may "change" it, which I doubt. The teacher population will not allow change, and this system will continue to be outdated. Great place for social interaction, but a bad one to get results, or to achieve someone's goal.  
>   
> Sad part is that the majority of people will not watch this video, will spend money an time going to school, and will learn nothing. Zero return in their investment. We can only spread the word of self education, and a hunger for knowledge that benefit our lives.

I had a professor at Rutgers University in the Puerto Rican Studies Dept. and he spoke a lot about many of these topics. His name is Andres Perez Jimenez. We loved attending and looked forward to his lectures and class discussions. He used the EIAG method. 

The EIAG curriculum framework in university settings is an experiential learning model—Experience, Identify, Analyze, Generalize—designed to help students actively process and apply learning from practical activities, simulations, or role-plays. It shifts focus from passive lecturing to student-centered, reflective, and skills-based learning.

### **@Preussischblau9373** | 👍 7 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=Ugxb7qeWi9SMLFSgs6p4AaABAg)

I have to disagree with the professor here. Teachers are not part of the power structure.

Teachers don’t make policy. Administration does. Parents also have power because they can pressure administrators and drive decisions. Teachers, on the other hand, have very little real authority over how the system actually runs.

What’s missing from the professor’s argument is that teachers are the only group in the system stuck in a built-in contradiction. They’re expected to deliver real learning and maintain standards, while at the same time complying with administrative demands that are often driven by parent satisfaction and optics rather than pedagogy. Those two things frequently conflict.

Everyone else operates around a single goal. Admin wants to keep the community happy. Families and students often want high grades whether the learning is there or not. Colleges want enrollment, money, and prestige. Teachers are the only ones asked to do the impossible: provide meaningful instruction while working inside constraints designed to avoid complaints and keep numbers looking good.

Teachers are not the ones pushing to water down the curriculum or pass students who haven’t learned the material. In many cases, they’re pressured to inflate grades or lower expectations, or they end up in conflict with administration. That’s how you end up with grade inflation and standards slipping.

If anything, teachers are the only group trying to hold onto some professional and ethical ground while navigating a system that constantly pulls in the opposite direction.

Teachers aren’t running this system. They’re surviving it. And a lot of the burnout and turnover comes from being stuck in that double bind every day.

One more thing worth noticing: people move from teaching into administration all the time, and they rarely go back. That tells you where the power actually sits.

> **@OlafsonN**

I was blessed to go to a super progressive quaker elementary school for a few years before middle school - they totally were teaching us from a young age in a way the Prof would love - unfortunately the founder died soon after i left and the remaining collective wasn't able to carry on the schools vision and finances and it basically collapsed- but i carried that renegade love for learning and real thinking in my heart like a torch - we were even taught conflict resolution and how to listen and respect one another from day one as the most important aspect of learning - it made my mind very aware of the brutal systematized structure of school and our culture once i went back into public schools and that awareness led to a natural sense of game theory and thank GoDD for that !! Sadly i watched most of my cohorts from that school slowly get grinded down over the years - this whole civilization is like a living, breathing meat grinder! But i can say game theory awareness saved my life and helped me dip in and out the game on my own terms! Thank you Prof Ji for offering this critical life skill to a new generation - THEY ARE GONNA NEED IT!!!

### **@garophelaus** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=UgxGEx2foq5cNvIxWeR4AaABAg)

Finland's schools aren't as good as they used to be and their quality is headed downwards. They are still 'ok' because the rate of degradation isn't too bad and the starting point was pretty high. I think one reason is that the teachers no longer have as much time for teaching due to an increasing amount of mandated reporting about the teaching. Another reason is that for a long time teachers couldn't do much about students using their smartphones during class, I believe that has now improved. Another possible reason is that there were a few (probably ideologically motivated) changes to the education which weren't successful and the people in charge don't have the incentive to admit that. One final reason is the closing of many small schools which leads to larger classes in bigger schools and more time spent in transport.

### **@user-fl6ko9do5y**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=UgyhYab2-EfGHFEKKGd4AaABAg)

I freaking Love this guy!! You would have been one of my favorite friends in US if I met you in college and after. This is how I remember talking with my friends in the 90's. Today,  you sound like a homeschool parent intellectual. Luckily, I do know a few. Btw, schools in 1980's were pretty good in US too. My mom was a teacher for 30+years and stayed up grading papers and coming up with lectures and projects - 
Everyday, even through out summer, until 2 in the morning most nights, only to be the first,  arriving early to set up her classroom for her lectures and interactive projects, at 7am. All while raising 4 kids and helping us with our papers too. I have no idea how she did it. I'm not saying she was a nice parent; - but she was a damn good teacher and students and parents loved/hated her,... but then loved her till the end. Her biggest thorn in her side was the administration- they could make or break a teachers creativity and support with disruptive/dangerous  students. I myself had some crappy teachers, but I would say 80% had a real stake in their self worth with their ability to teach and motivate students. And we knew nothing of their political leanings and what they did outside the classroom. We had to pry and trick them into divulging personal information and thought it was a big deal if we had an inkling about anything. Things were different as a society back then and people took personal pride in doing well, I think. I will say, that my classes all expected a lot of reading and essay reflection and critical thinking essays on everything.  For me, it took a lot of time to find my groove writing, since I thought deeply on subjects and had a hard time articulating my thoughts on paper, albeit not aloud. They hadn't taught us a formula for writing like they do now really. So we had to figure it out as we went.  I had to learn to translate written formulas with thought and I was a horrible speller. So I am talking 5 or 6 drafts, handwritten, with a painful wrist,  before I got it acceptable. The teachers didn't have scanning programs or typed papers, they didn't give special treatment for anytime of learning disability, and they expected your best. They had no problem handing out low grades and points- no mercy. You had to swallow your pride and fix it until you got it right. They had to read and correct everything themselves, even with peoples penmanship- which by the way was part of your grade- if it was sloppy or stylized, it wasn't read- just failed. It was tough but we learned to self critic well and iron did sharpen iron. I believe "fast education" like "fast fashion" has made people speed up and spit out more, but with less comprehension and depth, from student to teacher. We all want something for nothing- but yet in the real reflection- is it costs us everything. The curiosity and process of learning is so important. We must love to learn, but give the time and space to do so. With everyone on line all the time, are we really doing this?

---

First off, thank you for sharing your lectures online for free. They are excellent - in both their content and delivery.

I have been analyzing organizational design through a gamified lens for several years. My research has led to a gamified framework for launching and operating tiny, sustainable, humanist orgs. My working title for this framework is Employment as a Game Service (EaaGS).

So far, my focus has been on indie technology, small business, and non-profit orgs. Some of my discoveries will be obvious, but some have been surprising.

Education is my next area of research. If you happen to have the time, I'd love to touch base and ask some questions. Not an expectation – just an invitation.

Thanks again!

### **@RoyAlvarezLuna**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=Ugx8_yCctgLQz-Tqu5Z4AaABAg)

I was never recommended books to read by teachers, I wasn't paid attention to because I went to a 96% eurasian school, and the teachers/school decided to separate us and teach us to be servants; that's it. 

Luckily I was already inquisitive enough naturally that I began to read before i started school, and was a prolific reader by middle school or 7-8th grade in the US, or 12/13 years old. 

That's when history classes really started for most and I was already too aware of history that I became argumentative, and so ignored. Math teachers told me I would never need math in my life, English and art teachers would say to me, you can do anything you want, you will get an A+. I was good at being creative in several ways by that time, and those two subjects became my focus. 
Otherwise, every subject and every teacher ignored me, as if they knew to do so, which I later learned from a cousin who's a school administrator, is in fact the case. 

I knew they were ignoring me, it wasn't very much a secrete, just told with a smile and double language. 

The best thing I ever did, in my case, was not pay attention in school. 

When I told my cousin, the school administrator, that I was starting a business with a few partners she said to me, you can't do that, you need to be an administrator to manage a company, I responded with, I am not an administrator, that's boring! you do that! that's what you like to do! so I will hire someone like you to do that work while I own and lead the business which you know nothing about. She looked at me like 😳. 

Yeah bitch, you thought I was an idiot like the rest of the system 🤣
Now I own 2 businesses in 2 states with business managers running them while I build relationships with vendors and buyers. She's still dealing with little shits, that may shoot up the school🤷🏿. 

Fuck the western school systems.

---

### **@xlerb_again_to_music7908**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=UgxR0LcIbmsfc4ui7Id4AaABAg)

Growing up in the UK after WW2 and going through state education, little of this applied (that I can see). Education did not involve ££ outside of government; all was free to the student and family (indeed as a student, I was always in receipt of a modest stipend). Plus, public standing ("face") was not an issue; the country was struggling to get on its feet after the war. There were plenty of jobs, skills were short. Competition was to a standard, not between students/people. Nearly all faces were one colour, because over 99.9% of the population were caucasians (I was not in a big city).

Clearly, context matters!

On the topic of superstructures, there's this assumption I've had that the quality of life of each individual (access to food, water, health facilities etc) is dependent on the maturity stage of the society. But these factors also are tied heavily with the development of technology, which I imagine would not completely move in adherence with the maturity of a society (imagine if the modern world was to collapse, our technologies should not be completely lost and we would not have to start from sticks and stones). Would it be more accurate then to suggest that quality of life depends on technology and thus could be independent of the society's stage of maturity?

This then leads to my main point which is, would it be possible for the populace to live "easy high quality" lives even at the early or middling stages of societal maturity through the preservation of technology through social collapse?

To counter my own point, perhaps the size of the society limits how advanced technology can be, as insufficient power or workers are able to be supplied by small infantile societies to support the technologies that would enable the populace to live easy lives.

Any opinions?

### **@asteria4279**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=UgwyDynNnx2t7DasIdZ4AaABAg)

This is a critique of the Chinese education system, not "school" as a whole concept around the world. As a teacher in Canada, many of the premises outlined at the start are simply not true here. For example, every English class does a novel study and reading time. Every course (including math!) has content on critical thinking. Class ranking is not even a thing here. I might privately tell a student that they got the top mark, but marks aren't posted publicly anymore, and there hasn't been class rankings in my lifetime (and I'm middle aged). Grades are not a zero-sum game here - if half the class can get 98% on a legitimate test, than half the class gets that. (Goes the other way too lol)
Game theory is fascinating, but it's hard to take this lecture seriously when he and many commenters are conflagrating Chinese school and educational culture with ALL schools and cultures.

But most things you need to do to change the world as a normal good honest human being require large scale cooperation because the world is huge and its impossible to do it alone. That's why, for example, we cannot solve the problems of human driven climate change. Despite the fact we know exactly how this works, why this happens and how to solve it, we can't agree to solve it because the incentive structure is adverse to it. And until the severe effects will not hurt first hand a critical mass of people, nobody cares or we choose to believe in fairy tales that justifies it in another way. Basically blame everything else, but the problem itself which is burning fossil fuels and emit way too much co2 into the atmosphere.

### **@U_see-ph**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kS-muAuq62E&lc=UgyZgwgxwFnThWKEQHR4AaABAg)

With all due respect, this approach to teaching feels overly simplistic to a Russian-speaking audience. The professor’s speech is noticeably simplified due to the cultural and linguistic context—he explains basic ideas in very plain terms. In our universities, we’re not used to hearing statements like “school is bad” framed in such a simplistic way. In the Russian academic tradition, this topic would be explored in much greater depth, with a wider range of complex concepts and, most importantly, precise terminology. It’s enough to compare Russian and American literature (Dostoevsky vs Mark Twain) to understand what I find lacking in the lectures of the respected professor.
